[BODY] see that no man is excepted; all are enjoined to be subject, and to take their own places in the body.

[BODY] What aspect then does Popery present, but that of a crooked, deformed person? Is not the whole symmetry of the church destroyed, when one man, acting in opposition to the head, refuses to be reckoned one of the members? The Papists deny this, and allege that the Pope is nothing more than a ministerial head. But such cavils do them no service. The tyranny of their idol must be acknowledged to be altogether inconsistent with that order which Paul here recommends. In a word, a healthful condition of the church requires that Christ alone “must increase,” and all others “must decrease.” (<sty c="800000" i="0"><430330></sty>John 3:30.) Whatever increase we obtain must be regulated in such a manner, that we shall remain in our own place, and contribute to exalt the head.

[BODY] When he bids us give heed to the truth <sty c="000000" i="1">in love,</sty> he uses the preposition <sty c="000000" i="1">in</sty>, (<sty c="0000d4" i="0">ejn</sty>,) like the corresponding Hebrew preposition <sty c="008080" i="0">ב</sty>, (<sty c="000000" i="1">beth</sty>,) as signifying <sty c="000000" i="1">with</sty>, — <sty c="800000" i="1">speaking the truth With love</sty>. <sty c="800000" i="0">F41</sty> If each individual, instead of attending exclusively to his own concerns, shall desire mutual intercourse, there will be agreeable and general progress. Such, the Apostle assures us, must be the nature of this harmony, that men shall not be suffered to forget the claims of truth, or, disregarding them, to frame an agreement according to their own views. This proves the wickedness of the Papists, who lay aside the word of God, and labor to force our compliance with their decisions.

[BODY] 16. <sty c="800000" i="1">From whom the whole body.</sty> All our increase should tend to exalt more highly the glory of Christ. This is now proved by the best possible reason. It is he who supplies all our wants, and without whose protection we cannot be safe. As the root conveys sap to the whole tree, so all the vigor which we possess must flow to us from Christ. There are three things here which deserve our attention. The first is what has now been stated. All the life or health which is diffused through the members flows from the head; so that the members occupy a subordinate rank. The second is, that, by the distribution made, the limited share of each renders the communication between all the members absolutely necessary. The third is, that, without mutual love, the health of the body cannot be maintained. Through the members, as canals, is conveyed from the head all that is necessary for the nourishment of the body. While this connection is upheld, the body is

--- PAGE 93 ---

[BODY] alive and healthy. Each member, too, has its own proper share, — <sty c="800000" i="1">according to the effectual working in the measure of every part.</sty>

[BODY] Lastly, he shows that by love the church is edified, — <sty c="800000" i="1">to the edifying of</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">itself in love.</sty> This means that no increase is advantageous, which does not bear a just proportion to the whole body. That man is mistaken who desires his own separate growth. If a leg or arm should grow to a prodigious size, or the mouth be more fully distended, would the undue enlargement of those parts be otherwise than injurious to the whole frame? In like manner, if we wish to be considered members of Christ, let no man be anything for himself, but let us all be whatever we are for the benefit of each other. This is accomplished by love; and where it does not reign, there is no “edification,” but an absolute scattering of the church.

[H2] <sty c="800000" i="0"><490417></sty>EPHESIANS 4:17-19

[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 17|||EN|||This I say therefore, and testify in the Lord, that ye henceforth walk not as other Gentiles walk, in the vanity of their mind;|||LA|||Hoc ergo dico et testificor in Domino, ne ambuletis amplius, quemadmodum et gentes reliquae ambulant
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 18|||EN|||Having the understanding darkened, being alienated from the life of God through the ignorance that is in them, because of the blindness of their heart:|||LA|||In vanitate mentis suae, excaecatae in intelligentia, alienatae a vita Dei propter ignorantiam, quae in illis est, propter caecitatem cordis earum;
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 19|||EN|||Who, being past feeling, have given themselves over unto lasciviousness, to work all uncleanness with greediness.|||LA|||Quae postquam dolore tangi desierunt, seipsas tradiderunt lasciviae, ad perpetrandam omnem immunditiam cum aviditate.
[BODY] 17.<sty c="800000" i="1"> This I say therefore.</sty> That government which Christ has appointed for the edification of his church has now been considered. He next inquires what fruits the doctrine of the gospel ought to yield in the lives of Christians; or, if you prefer it, he begins to explain minutely the nature of that edification by which doctrine ought to be followed.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">That ye henceforth walk not in vanity.</sty> He first exhorts them to renounce the <sty c="800000" i="1">vanity</sty> of unbelievers, arguing from its inconsistency with their present views. That those who have been taught in the school of Christ, and

--- PAGE 94 ---

[BODY] enlightened by the doctrine of salvation, should follow vanity, and in no respect differ from those unbelieving and blind nations on whom no light of truth has ever shone, would be singularly foolish. On this ground he very properly calls upon them to demonstrate, by their life, that they had gained some advantage by becoming the disciples of Christ. To impart to his exhortation the greater earnestness, he beseeches them by the name of God, — <sty c="800000" i="1">this I say and testify in the Lord,</sty> <sty c="800000" i="0">F42</sty> — reminding them, that, if they despised this instruction, they must one day give an account.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">As other Gentiles walk.</sty> He means those who had not yet been converted to Christ. But, at the same time, he reminds the Ephesians how necessary it was that they should repent, since by nature they resembled lost and condemned men. The miserable and shocking condition of other nations is held out as the motive to a change of disposition, He asserts that believers differ from unbelievers; and points out, as we shall see, the causes of this difference. With regard to the former, he accuses their mind of <sty c="800000" i="1">vanity:</sty> and let us remember, that he speaks generally of all who have not been renewed by the Spirit of Christ.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">In the vanity of their mind.</sty> Now, <sty c="000000" i="1">the mind</sty> holds the highest rank in the human constitution, is the seat of reason, presides over the will, and restrains sinful desires; so that our theologians of the <sty c="000000" i="1">Sorbonne</sty> are in the habit of calling her the Queen. But, Paul makes the <sty c="000000" i="1">mind</sty> to consist of nothing else than vanity; and, as if he had not expressed his meaning strongly enough, he gives no better title to her daughter, <sty c="000000" i="1">the understanding.</sty> Such is my interpretation of the word <sty c="0000d4" i="0">dianoi>a</sty>; for, though it signifies the thought, yet, as it is in the singular number, it refers to the thinking faculty. Plato, about the close of his Sixth Book on a Republic, assigns to <sty c="0000d4" i="0">dianoi>a</sty> an intermediate place between <sty c="0000d4" i="0">no>hsiv</sty> and <sty c="0000d4" i="0">po>stiv</sty> but his observations are so entirely confined to geometrical subjects, as not to admit of application to this passage. Having formerly asserted that men see nothing, Paul now adds, that they are blind in reasoning, even on the most important subjects.

[BODY] Let men now go and be proud of free-will, whose guidance is here marked by so deep disgrace. But experience, we shall be told, is openly at variance with this opinion; for men are not so blind as to be incapable of seeing anything, nor so vain as to be incapable of forming any judgment. I answer, with respect to the kingdom of God, and all that relates to the spiritual life,

--- PAGE 95 ---

[BODY] the light of human reason differs little from darkness; for, before it has pointed out the road, it is extinguished; and its power of perception is little else than blindness, for ere it has reached the fruit it is gone. The true principles held by the human mind resemble sparks; <sty c="800000" i="0">F43</sty> but; these are choked by the depravity of our nature, before they have been applied to their proper use. All men know, for instance, that there is a God, and that it is our duty to worship him; but such is the power of sin and ignorance, that from this confused knowledge we pass all at once to an idol, and worship it in the place of God. And even in the worship of God, it leads to great errors, particularly in the first table of the law.

[BODY] As to the second objection, our judgment does indeed agree with the law of God in regard to the mere outward actions; but sinful desire, which is the source of everything evil, escapes our notice. Besides, Paul does not speak merely of the natural blindness which we brought with us from the womb, but refers also to a still grosser blindness, by which, as we shall afterwards see, God punishes former transgressions. We conclude with observing, that the reason and understanding which men naturally possess, make them in the sight of God without excuse; but, so long as they allow themselves to live according to their natural disposition, they can only wander, and fall, and stumble in their purposes and actions. Hence it appears in what estimation and value false worship must appear in the sight of God, when it proceeds from the gulf of vanity and the maze of ignorance.

[BODY] 18. <sty c="800000" i="1">Being alienated from the life of God.</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">The life of God</sty> may either mean what is accounted life in the sight of God, as in that passage,

[CENTERED] “<sty c="000080" i="0">they loved the praise of men more than </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">the praise of God</sty><sty c="000080" i="0">,</sty>”
[CENTERED] <sty c="000080" i="0">(</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"><431243></sty><sty c="000080" i="0">John 12:43,)</sty>

[BODY] or, that life which God bestows on his elect by the Spirit of regeneration. In both cases the meaning is the same. Our ordinary life, as men, is nothing more than an empty image of life, not only because it quickly passes, but also because, while we live, our souls, not keeping close to God, are dead. There are three kinds of life in this world. The first is animal life, which consists only of motion and the bodily senses, and which we have in common with the brutes; the second is human life, which we have as the children of Adam; and the third is that supernatural life, which believers alone obtain. And all of them are from God, so that each of them may be called <sty c="800000" i="1">the life of God.</sty> As to the first, Paul, in his sermon at Athens, says,

--- PAGE 96 ---

[BODY] (<sty c="800000" i="0"><441728></sty>Acts 17:28,) “In him we live, and move, and have our being;” and the Psalmist says,

[CENTERED] “<sty c="000080" i="0">Send forth thy Spirit, and they shall be created; and thou wilt</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">renew the face of the earth.</sty>”<sty c="000080" i="0"> (</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"><19A430></sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Psalm 104:30.)</sty>

[BODY] Of the second Job says,

[CENTERED] “<sty c="000080" i="0">Thou hast granted me life, and thy visitation hath preserved my</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">spirit.</sty>”<sty c="000080" i="0"> (</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"><181012></sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Job 10:12.)</sty>

[BODY] But the regeneration of believers is here called, by way of eminence, <sty c="800000" i="1">the life</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">of God, </sty>because then does God truly live in us, and we enjoy his life, when he governs us by his Spirit. Of this life all men who are not new creatures in Christ are declared by Paul to be destitute. So long, then, as we remain in the flesh, that is, in ourselves, how wretched must be our condition! We may now form a judgment of all the moral virtues, as they are called; for what sort of actions will that life produce which, Paul affirms, is not the life of God? Before anything good can begin to proceed from us, we must first be renewed by the grace of Christ. This will be the commencement of a true, and, as the phrase is, a <sty c="000000" i="1">vital</sty> life.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">On account of the ignorance that is in them.</sty> We ought to attend to the reason which is here assigned; for, as the knowledge of God is the true life of the soul, so, on the contrary, ignorance is the death of it. And lest we should adopt the opinion of philosophers, that ignorance, which leads us into mistakes, is only an incidental evil, Paul shews that it has its root <sty c="800000" i="1">in</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">the blindness of their heart,</sty> by which he intimates that it dwells in their very nature. The first blindness, therefore, which covers the minds of men, is the punishment of original sin; because Adam, after his revolt, was deprived of the true light of God, in the absence of which there is nothing but fearful darkness.

[BODY] 19. <sty c="800000" i="1">Who being past feeling.</sty> The account which had been given of natural depravity is followed by a description of the worst of all evils, brought upon men by their own sinful conduct. Having destroyed the sensibilities of the heart, and allayed the stings of remorse, they abandon themselves to all manner of iniquity. We are by nature corrupt and prone to evil; nay, we are wholly inclined to evil. Those who are destitute of the Spirit of Christ give loose reins to self-indulgence, till fresh offenses, producing others in constant succession, bring down upon them the wrath of God. The voice

--- PAGE 97 ---

[BODY] of God, proclaimed by an accusing conscience, still continues to be heard; but, instead of producing its proper effects, appears rather to harden them against all admonition. On account of such obstinacy, they deserve to be altogether forsaken by God.

[BODY] The usual symptom of their having been thus forsaken is — the insensibility to pain, which is here described — <sty c="800000" i="1">being past feeling</sty>. Unmoved by the approaching judgment of God, whom they offend, they go on at their ease, and fearlessly indulge without restraint in the pleasures of sin. No shame is felt, no regard to character is maintained. The gnawing of a guilty conscience, tormented by the dread of the Divine judgment, may be compared to the porch of hell; but such hardened security as this is a whirlpool which swallows up and destroys. As Solomon says,

[CENTERED] “<sty c="000080" i="0">When the wicked is come to the deep, he despiseth it.</sty>”
[CENTERED] <sty c="000080" i="0">(</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"><201803></sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Proverbs 18:3.)</sty>

[BODY] Most properly, therefore, does Paul exhibit that dreadful example of Divine vengeance, in which men forsaken by God — having laid conscience to sleep, and destroyed all fear of the Divine judgment, — in a word, <sty c="800000" i="1">being</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">past feeling, </sty>— surrender themselves with brutal violence to all wickedness. This is not universally the case. Many even of the reprobate are restrained by God, whose infinite goodness prevents the absolute confusion in which the world would otherwise be involved. The consequence is, that such open lust, such unrestrained intemperance, does not appear in all. It is enough that the lives of some present such a mirror, fitted to awaken our alarm lest anything similar should happen to ourselves.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">Lasciviousness</sty> (<sty c="0000d4" i="0">ajselgei>a|</sty>) appears to me to denote that wantonness with which the flesh indulges in intemperance and licentiousness, when not restrained by the Spirit of God. <sty c="800000" i="1">Uncleanness</sty> is put for scandalous enormities of every description. It is added, <sty c="800000" i="1">with greediness</sty>. The Greek word <sty c="0000d4" i="0">pleonexi>a</sty>, which is so translated, often signifies covetousness, (<sty c="800000" i="0"><421215></sty>Luke 12:15; <sty c="800000" i="0"><610214></sty>2 Peter 2:14,) and is so explained by some in this passage; but I cannot adopt that view. Depraved and wicked desires being insatiable, Paul represents them as attended and followed by <sty c="800000" i="1">greediness</sty>, which is the contrary of moderation.

--- PAGE 98 ---

[H2] <sty c="800000" i="0"><490420></sty>EPHESIANS 4:20-24

[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 20|||EN|||But ye have not so learned Christ;|||LA|||Vos autem non ita didicistis Christum;
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 21|||EN|||If so be that ye have heard him, and have been taught by him, as the truth is in Jesus:|||LA|||Si quidem ipsum audistis, et in ipso estis edocti, quemadmodum est veritas in Iesu;
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 22|||EN|||That ye put off, concerning the former conversation, the old man, which is corrupt according to the deceitful lusts;|||LA|||Ut deponatis, secundum pristinam conversationem, Veterem hominem, qui corrumpitur secundum concupiscentias erroris;
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 23|||EN|||And be renewed in the spirit of your mind;|||LA|||Renovemini autem spiritu mentis vestrae
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 24|||EN|||And that ye put on the new man, which after God is created in righteousness and true holiness.|||LA|||Et induatis Novum hominem, qui secundum Deum creatus est, in justitia et sanctitate veritatis.
[BODY] 20. <sty c="800000" i="1">But ye have not.</sty> He now draws a contrast of a Christian life, so as to make it evident how utterly inconsistent it is with the character of a godly man to defile himself regardlessly with the abominations of the Gentiles. Because the Gentiles walk in darkness, therefore they do not distinguish between right and wrong; but those on whom the truth of God shines ought to live in a different manner. That those to whom the vanity of the senses is a rule of life, should yield themselves up to base lusts, is not surprising; but the doctrine of Christ teaches us to renounce our natural dispositions. He whose life differs not from that of unbelievers, has learned nothing of Christ; for the knowledge of Christ cannot be separated from the mortification of the flesh.

[BODY] 21. <sty c="800000" i="1">If ye have heard him.</sty> To excite their attention and earnestness the more, he not only tells them that they had <sty c="800000" i="1">heard Christ,</sty> but employs a still stronger expression, <sty c="800000" i="1">ye have been taught in him,</sty> as if he had said, that this doctrine had not been slightly pointed out, but faithfully delivered and explained.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">As the truth is in Jesus.</sty> This contains a reproof of that superficial knowledge of the gospel, by which many are elated, who are wholly

--- PAGE 99 ---

[BODY] unacquainted with newness of life. They think that they are exceedingly wise, but the apostle pronounces it to be a false and mistaken opinion. There is a twofold knowledge of Christ, one, which is true and genuine, and another, which is counterfeit and spurious. Not that, strictly speaking, there are two kinds; but most men falsely imagine that they know Christ, while they know nothing but what is carnal. In another Epistle he says,

[CENTERED] “<sty c="000080" i="0">If any man be in Christ, let him be a new creature.</sty>”
[CENTERED] <sty c="000080" i="0">(</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"><470517></sty><sty c="000080" i="0">2 Corinthians 5:17.)</sty>

[BODY] So here he affirms that any knowledge of Christ, which is not accompanied by mortification of the flesh, is not true and sincere.

[BODY] 22. <sty c="800000" i="1">That ye put off.</sty> He demands from a Christian man repentance, or a new life, which he makes to consist of self-denial and the regeneration of the Holy Spirit. Beginning with the first, he enjoins us to lay aside, or <sty c="800000" i="1">put off</sty> the old man, employing the metaphor of garments, which we have already had occasion to explain. <sty c="800000" i="1">The old man,</sty> — as we have repeatedly stated, in expounding the sixth chapter of the Epistle to the Romans, and other passages where it occurs, means the natural disposition which we bring with us from our mother’s womb. In two persons, Adam and Christ, he describes to us what may be called two natures. As we are first born of Adam, the depravity of nature which we derive from him is called the <sty c="000000" i="1">Old</sty> man; and as we are born again in Christ, the amendment of this sinful nature is called the <sty c="000000" i="1">New</sty> man. In a word, he who desires to put off the old man must renounce his nature. To suppose that the words <sty c="800000" i="1">Old</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"> </sty>and<sty c="800000" i="1"> New</sty> contain an allusion to the Old and New Testaments, is exceedingly unphilosophical.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">Concerning the former conversation.</sty> To make it more evident that this exhortation to the Ephesians was not unnecessary, he reminds them of their former life. “Before Christ revealed himself to your minds, the old man reigned in you; and therefore, if you desire to lay him aside, you must renounce your former life.” <sty c="800000" i="1">Which is corrupted.</sty> He describes the old man from the fruits, that is, from the wicked desires, which allure men to destruction; for the word, <sty c="800000" i="1">corrupt,</sty> alludes to old age, which is closely allied to corruption. Let us beware of considering <sty c="800000" i="1">the deceitful lusts,</sty> as the Papists do, to mean nothing more than the gross and visible lusts, which are generally acknowledged to be base. The word includes also those dispositions which, instead of being censured, are sometimes applauded,

--- PAGE 100 ---

[BODY] such as ambition, cunning, and everything that proceeds either from selflove or from want of confidence in God.

[BODY] 23.<sty c="800000" i="1"> And be renewed.</sty> The second part of the rule for a devout and holy life is to live, not in our own spirit, but in the Spirit of Christ. But what is meant by — <sty c="800000" i="1">the spirit of your mind?</sty> I understand it simply to mean, — <sty c="800000" i="1">Be</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">renewed,</sty> not only with respect to the inferior appetites or desires, which are manifestly sinful, but with respect also to that part of the soul which is reckoned most noble and excellent. And here again, he brings forward to view that Queen which philosophers are accustomed almost to adore. There is an implied contrast between <sty c="800000" i="1">the spirit of our mind</sty> and the Divine and heavenly Spirit, who produces in us another and a new mind. How much there is in us that is sound or uncorrupted may be easily gathered from this passage, which enjoins us to correct chiefly the reason or <sty c="800000" i="1">mind,</sty> in which we are apt to imagine that there is nothing but what is virtuous and deserves commendation.

[BODY] 24. <sty c="800000" i="1">And that ye put on the new man.</sty> All that is meant is, “Be renewed in the spirit, or, be renewed <sty c="000000" i="1">within</sty> or <sty c="000000" i="1">completely,</sty> — beginning with the mind, which appears to be the part most free from all taint of sin.” What is added about the creation, may refer either to the first creation of man, or to the second creation, which is effected by the grace of Christ. Both expositions will be true. Adam was at first created after the image of God, and reflected, as in a mirror, the Divine righteousness; but that image, having been defaced by sin, must now be restored in Christ. The regeneration of the godly is indeed as we have formerly explained <sty c="800000" i="0">F44</sty> — nothing else than the formation anew of the image of God in them. There is, no doubt, a far more rich and powerful manifestation of Divine grace in this second creation than in the first; but our highest perfection is uniformly represented in Scripture as consisting in our conformity and resemblance to God. Adam lost the image which he had originally received, and therefore it becomes necessary that it shall be restored to us by Christ. The design contemplated by regeneration is to recall us from our wanderings to that end for which we were created.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">In righteousness.</sty> If <sty c="800000" i="1">righteousness</sty> be taken as a general term for uprightness, <sty c="800000" i="1">holiness</sty> will be something higher, or that purity which lies in being devoted to the service of God. I am rather inclined to consider

--- PAGE 101 ---

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">holiness</sty> as referring to the first table, and <sty c="800000" i="1">righteousness</sty> to the second table, of the law, as in the song of Zacharias,

[CENTERED] “<sty c="000080" i="0">That we may serve him in </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">holiness and righteousness</sty><sty c="000080" i="0">, all the days of our</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">life.</sty>”<sty c="000080" i="0"> (</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"><420174></sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Luke 1:74,75.)</sty>

[BODY] Plato lays down the distinction correctly, that holiness (<sty c="0000d4" i="0">oJsio>thv</sty>) lies in the worship of God, and that the other part,<sty c="800000" i="1"> righteousness,</sty> (<sty c="0000d4" i="0">dikaiosu>nh</sty>,) bears a reference to men. The genitive, <sty c="000000" i="1">of truth</sty>, (<sty c="0000d4" i="0">th~v</sty> <sty c="0000d4" i="0">alhqei>av</sty>,) is put in the place of an adjective, and refers to both terms; so that, while it literally runs, <sty c="800000" i="1">in righteousness and holiness of truth,</sty> the meaning is, <sty c="000000" i="1">in true righteousness and</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">holiness.</sty> He warns us that both ought to be sincere; because we have to do with God, whom it is impossible to deceive.

[H2] <sty c="800000" i="0"><490425></sty>EPHESIANS 4:25-28

[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 25|||EN|||Wherefore, putting away lying, speak every man truth with his neighbor: for we are members one of another.|||LA|||Quare, deposito mendacio, loquimini veritatem unusquisque cum proximo suo; quia sumus vicissim inter nos membra.
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 26|||EN|||Be ye angry, and sin not: let not the sun go down upon your wrath:|||LA|||Irascimini, et ne peccetis. (<sty c="800000" i="0"><190405></sty>Psalm 4:5.) Sol non occidat super iracundiam vestram:
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 27|||EN|||Neither give place to the devil.|||LA|||Et ne detis locum diabolo.
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 28|||EN|||Let him that stole steal no more: but rather let him labor, working with his hands the thing which is good, that he may have to give to him that needeth.|||LA|||Qui furabatur, jam non furetur; magis autem laboret, operando quod bonum est manibus, ut habeat quod eroget opus habenti.
[BODY] 25. <sty c="800000" i="1">Wherefore, putting away lying.</sty> From this head of doctrine, that is, from the righteousness of the new man, all godly exhortations flow, like streams from a fountain; for if all the precepts which relate to life were collected, yet, without this principle, they would be of little value. Philosophers take a different method; but, in the doctrine of godliness, there is no other way than this for regulating the life. Now, therefore, he comes to lay down particular exhortations, drawn from the general doctrine. Having concluded

--- PAGE 102 ---

[BODY] from the truth of the gospel, that righteousness and holiness ought to be true, he now argues from the general statement to a particular instance, that <sty c="800000" i="1">every man should speak truth with his neigbbour.</sty><sty c="000000" i="1"> </sty><sty c="800000" i="1">Lying</sty> is here put for every kind of deceit, hypocrisy, or cunning; and <sty c="800000" i="1">truth</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"> </sty>for honest dealing. He demands that every kind of communication between them shall be sincere; and enforces it by this consideration, for we are members one of another. That <sty c="800000" i="1">members</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"> </sty>should not agree among themselves, that they should act in a deceitful manner towards each other, is prodigious wickedness.

[BODY] 26. <sty c="800000" i="1">Be ye angry, and sin not.</sty> Whether or not the apostle had in his eye a part of the fourth Psalm is uncertain. The words used by him (<sty c="0000d4" i="0">jOrgi>zesqe</sty> <sty c="0000d4" i="0">kai< uh< aJmarta>nete</sty>) occur in the Greek translation, though the word <sty c="0000d4" i="0">ojrgi>zesqe</sty>, which is translated, <sty c="800000" i="1">be ye angry,</sty> is considered by some to mean <sty c="000000" i="1">tremble. </sty><sty c="800000" i="0">F45</sty> The Hebrew verb <sty c="008080" i="0">רגז</sty> (<sty c="000000" i="1">ragaz</sty>) signifies either to be agitated by anger, or, to tremble. As to the passage of the Psalm, the idea of <sty c="800000" i="1">trembling</sty> will be quite appropriate. “Do not choose to resemble madmen, who rush fearlessly in any direction, but let the dread of being accounted foolhardy keep you in awe.” The word sometimes signifies to <sty c="000000" i="1">strive</sty> or <sty c="000000" i="1">quarrel</sty>, as, in that instance, (<sty c="800000" i="0"><014524></sty>Genesis 45:24,) “See that ye <sty c="000000" i="1">fall not out</sty> by the way;” and accordingly, the Psalmist adds, “Commune with your own heart, and be still,” — abstain from furious encounters.

[BODY] In my opinion, Paul merely alludes to the passage with the following view. There are three faults by which we offend God in being angry. The first is, when our anger arises from slight causes, and often from no cause whatever, or at least from private injuries or offenses. The second is, when we go beyond the proper bounds, and are hurried into intemperate excesses. The third is, when our anger, which ought to have been directed against ourselves or against sins, is turned against our brethren. Most appropriately, therefore, did Paul, when he wished to describe the proper limitation of anger, employ the well-known passage<sty c="800000" i="1">, Be ye angry, and sin</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">not. </sty>We comply with this injunction, if the objects of our anger are sought, not in others, but in ourselves, — if we pour out our indignation against our own faults. With respect to others, we ought to be angry, not at their persons, but at their faults; nor ought we to be excited to anger by private offenses, but by zeal for the glory of the Lord. Lastly, our anger, after a reasonable time, ought to be allowed to subside, without mixing itself with the violence of carnal passions.

--- PAGE 103 ---

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">Let not the sun go down.</sty> It is scarcely possible, however, but that we shall sometimes give way to improper and sinful passion, so strong is the tendency of the human mind to what is evil. Paul therefore suggests a second remedy, that we shall quickly suppress our anger, and not suffer it to gather strength by continuance. The first remedy was, <sty c="800000" i="1">Be ye angry, and</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">sin not;</sty> but, as the great weakness of human nature renders this exceedingly difficult, the next is — not to cherish wrath too long in our minds, or allow it sufficient time to become strong. He enjoins accordingly, <sty c="800000" i="1">let not the sun go down upon your wrath.</sty> If at any time we happen to be angry, let us endeavor to be appeased before the sun has set.

[BODY] 27. <sty c="800000" i="1">Neither give place</sty> (<sty c="0000d4" i="0">tw~| diabo>lw|</sty>) <sty c="800000" i="1">to the devil.</sty> I am aware of the interpretation which some give of this passage. Erasmus, who translates it, “neither give place to the Slanderer,” (<sty c="000000" i="1">calumniatori,</sty>) shews plainly that he understood it as referring to malicious men. But I have no doubt, Paul’s intention was, to guard us against allowing Satan to take possession of our minds, and, by keeping in his hands this citadel, to do whatever he pleases. We feel every day how impossible, or, at least, how difficult it is to cure long-continued hatred. What is the cause of this, but that, instead of resisting the devil, we yield up to him the possession of our heart? Before the poison of hatred has found its way into the heart, anger must be thoroughly dislodged.

[BODY] 28. <sty c="800000" i="1">Let him that stole steal no more.</sty> This includes not merely the grosser thefts which are punished by human laws, but those of a more concealed nature, which do not fall under the cognizance of men, every kind of depredation by which we seize the property of others. But he does not simply forbid us to take that property in an unjust or unlawful manner. He enjoins us to assist our brethren, as far as lies in our power.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">That he may have to give to him that needeth.</sty> “Thou who formerly stolest must not only obtain thy subsistence by lawful and harmless toil, but must give assistance to others.” He is first required <sty c="000000" i="1">to</sty><sty c="800000" i="1"> labor, working with</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">his hands,</sty> that he may not supply his wants at the expense of his brethren, but may support life by honorable labor. But the love which we owe to our neighbor carries us much farther. No one must live to himself alone, and neglect others. All must labor to supply each other’s necessities.

--- PAGE 104 ---

[BODY] But a question arises, does Paul oblige all men to labor with their hands? This would be excessively hard. I reply, the meaning is plain, if it be duly considered. Every man is forbidden to steal. But many people are in the habit of pleading want, and that excuse is obviated by enjoining <sty c="800000" i="1">them</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">rather to labor</sty> (<sty c="0000d4" i="0">ma~llon de kopia>tw</sty>) with their hands. As if he had said, “No condition, however hard or disagreeable, can entitle any man to do injury to another, or even to refrain from contributing to the necessities of his brethren.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">The thing which is good.</sty> This latter clause, which contains an argument from the greater to the less, gives no small additional strength to the exhortation. As there are many occupations which do little to promote the lawful enjoyments of men, he recommends to them to choose those employments which yield the greatest advantage to their neighbors. We need not wonder at this. If those trades which can have no other effect than to lead men into immorality, were denounced by heathens — and Cicero among the number — as highly disgraceful, would an apostle of Christ reckon them among the lawful callings of God?

[H2] <sty c="800000" i="0"><490429></sty>EPHESIANS 4:29-31


[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 29|||EN|||Let no corrupt communication proceed out of your mouth, but that which is good to the use of edifying, that it may minister grace unto the hearers.|||LA|||Omnis sermo spurcus ex ore vestro non procedat; sed si quis est bonus ad edificationem usus, ut det gratiam audientibus.
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 30|||EN|||And grieve not the Holy Spirit of God, whereby ye are sealed unto the day of redemption.|||LA|||Et ne contristetis Spiritum Sanctum Dei, quo obsignati estis in diem redemptionis.
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 31|||EN|||Let all bitterness, and wrath, and anger, and clamor, and evil-speaking, be put away from you, with all malice.|||LA|||Omnis amarulentia, et indignatio, et ira, et clamor, et maledicentia, removeatur a vobis cum omni malitia.
[BODY] 29. <sty c="800000" i="1">No filthy speech.</sty> He first forbids believers to use any <sty c="800000" i="1">filthy</sty> language, including under this name all those expressions which are wont to be employed for the purpose of inflaming lust. Not satisfied with the removal of the vice, he enjoins them to frame their discourse for edification. In

--- PAGE 105 ---

[BODY] another Epistle he says, “Let your speech be seasoned with salt.” (<sty c="800000" i="0"><510406></sty>Colossians 4:6.) Here a different phrase is employed, <sty c="800000" i="1">if any</sty> (speech) <sty c="800000" i="1">be good to the use of edifying,</sty> which means simply, <sty c="000000" i="1">if it be useful.</sty> The genitive, <sty c="000000" i="1">of </sty><sty c="800000" i="1">use,</sty> may no doubt be viewed, according to the Hebrew idiom, as put for an adjective, so that <sty c="800000" i="1">for the edification of use</sty> (<sty c="0000d4" i="0">oijkodomh<n</sty> <sty c="0000d4" i="0">th~v crei>av</sty>) may mean <sty c="000000" i="1">for useful edification;</sty> but when I consider how frequently, and in how extensive a meaning, the metaphor of <sty c="800000" i="1">edifying</sty> occurs in Paul’s writings, I prefer the former exposition. <sty c="800000" i="1">The edification of</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">use</sty> will thus mean the progress of our edification, for to edify is to carry forward. To explain the manner in which this is done, he adds, <sty c="800000" i="1">that it may</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">impart grace to the hearers,</sty> meaning by the word <sty c="800000" i="1">grace,</sty> comfort, advice, and everything that aids the salvation of the soul.

[BODY] 30. <sty c="800000" i="1">And grieve not.</sty> As the Holy Spirit dwells in us, to him every part of our soul and of our body ought to be devoted. But if we give ourselves up to aught that is impure, we may be said to drive him away from making his abode with us; and, to express this still more familiarly, human affections, such as joy and grief, are ascribed to the Holy Spirit. <sty c="800000" i="0">F46</sty> Endeavour that the Holy Spirit may dwell cheerfully with you, as in a pleasant and joyful dwelling, and give him no occasion for grief. Some take a different view of it, that we grieve the Holy Spirit in others, when we offend by filthy language, or, in any other way, godly brethren, who are led by the Spirit of God. (<sty c="800000" i="0"><450814></sty>Romans 8:14.) Whatever is contrary to godliness is not only disrelished by godly ears, but is no sooner heard than it produces in them deep grief and pain. But that Paul’s meaning was different appears from what follows.

[BODY] By whom ye are sealed. As God has sealed us by his Spirit, we grieve him when we do not follow his guidance, but pollute ourselves by wicked passions. No language can adequately express this solemn truth, that the Holy Spirit rejoices and is glad on our account, when we are obedient to him in all things, and neither think nor speak anything, but what is pure and holy; and, on the other hand, is grieved, when we admit anything into our minds that is unworthy of our calling. Now, let any man reflect what shocking wickedness there must be in grieving the Holy Spirit to such a degree as to compel him to withdraw from us. The same mode of speaking is used by the prophet Isaiah , but in a different sense; for he merely says, that they “vexed his Holy Spirit,” (<sty c="800000" i="0"><236310></sty>Isaiah 63:10.) in the same sense in which we are accustomed to speak of vexing the mind of a man. <sty c="800000" i="1">By</sty>

--- PAGE 106 ---

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">whom ye are sealed.</sty> The Spirit of God is the seal, by which we are distinguished from the wicked, and which is impressed on our hearts as a sure evidence of adoption.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">Unto the day of redemption,</sty> — that is, till God conduct us into the possession of the promised inheritance. That day is usually called <sty c="800000" i="1">the day</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">of redemption,</sty> because we shall then be at length delivered out of all our afflictions. It is unnecessary to make any observations on this phrase, in addition to what have already been made in expounding <sty c="800000" i="0"><450823></sty>Romans 8:23, and <sty c="800000" i="0"><460130></sty>1 Corinthians 1:30. In this passage, the word <sty c="800000" i="1">sealed</sty> may have a different meaning from that which it usually bears, — that God has impressed his Spirit as his mark upon us, that he may recognize as his children those whom he perceives to bear that mark.

[BODY] 31. <sty c="800000" i="1">Let all bitterness.</sty> He again condemns anger; but, on the present occasion, views in connection with it those offenses by which it is usually accompanied, such as noisy disputes and reproaches. Between <sty c="000000" i="1">wrath and</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">anger</sty> (<sty c="0000d4" i="0">Qumo<n kai< ojrgh<n</sty>) there is little difference, except that the former denotes the power, and the latter the act; but here, the only difference is, that <sty c="800000" i="1">anger</sty> is a more sudden attack. The correction of all the rest will be greatly aided by the removal of <sty c="800000" i="1">malice.</sty> By this term he expresses that depravity of mind which is opposed to humanity and justice, and which is usually called <sty c="000000" i="1">malignity.</sty>

[H2] <sty c="800000" i="0"><490432></sty>EPHESIANS 4:32

[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 32|||EN|||And be ye kind one to another, tender-hearted, forgiving one another, even as God for Christ’s sake hath forgiven you.|||LA|||Sitis autem mutuo comes, misericordes, condonantes vobis inter vos, quemadmodum et Deus vobis in Christo condonavit.
[BODY] 32. <sty c="800000" i="1">And be ye kind one to another.</sty> With <sty c="800000" i="1">bitterness</sty> he contrasts <sty c="800000" i="1">kindness,</sty> or gentleness of countenance, language, and manners. The

--- PAGE 107 ---

[BODY] contrary of this is the cruelty of those iron-hearted, barbarous men, by whom the sufferings of others are beheld without any concern whatever.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">Forgiving one another.</sty> The Greek word here rendered<sty c="800000" i="1"> forgiving,</sty> (<sty c="0000d4" i="0">carizo>menoi eJautoi~v</sty>,) is supposed by to mean beneficence. That those whose kindness of heart in other respects disposes them to acts of humanity, may not fail in their duty through the ingratitude of men, he exhorts them to discover a readiness to lay aside resentment. To give his exhortation the greater weight, he holds out the example of God, who has forgiven to us, through Christ, far more than any mortal man can forgive to his brethren. <sty c="800000" i="0">F47</sty>

[H1] <sty c="0000d4" i="0">CHAPTER 5</sty>

[H2] <sty c="800000" i="0"><490501></sty>EPHESIANS 5:1-2

[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 1|||EN|||Be ye therefore followers of God, as dear children;|||LA|||Sitis ergo imitatores Dei quemadmodum filii dilecti;
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 2|||EN|||And walk in love, as Christ also hath loved us, and hath given himself for us an offering and a sacrifice to God for a sweet smelling savor.|||LA|||Et ambulate in charitate quemadmodum et Christus nos dilexit, ac se ipsum tradidit pro nobis oblationem et hostiam Deo, in odorem bonae fragrantiae.
[BODY] 1.<sty c="800000" i="1"> Be ye therefore followers.</sty> The same principle is followed out and enforced by the consideration that children ought to be like their father. He reminds us that we are the children of God, and that therefore we ought, as far as possible, to resemble Him in acts of kindness. It is impossible not to perceive, that the division of chapters, in the present instance, is particularly unhappy, as it has made a separation between parts of the subject which are very closely related. If, then, we are the children of God, we ought to be <sty c="800000" i="1">followers of God.</sty> Christ also declares, that, unless we shew kindness to the unworthy, we cannot be the children of our heavenly Father.

[CENTERED] “<sty c="000080" i="0">Love your enemies, bless them that curse you, do good to them</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">that hate you, and pray for them who despitefully use you and</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">persecute you; </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">that ye may be the children of your</sty><sty c="000080" i="0"> </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">Father which is</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">in heaven</sty><sty c="000080" i="0">; for he maketh his sun to rise on the evil and on the good,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">and sendeth rain on the just and on the unjust.</sty>” <sty c="000080" i="0">(</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"><400544></sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Matthew 5:44,45.) </sty><sty c="800000" i="0">F48</sty>

[BODY] 2. <sty c="800000" i="1">And walk in love as Christ also hath loved us.</sty> Having called on us to imitate God, he now calls on us to imitate Christ, who is our true model. We ought to embrace each other with that love with which Christ has embraced us, for what we perceive in Christ is our true guide.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">And gave himself for us.</sty> This was a remarkable proof of the highest love. Forgetful, as it were, of himself, Christ spared not his own life, that he

--- PAGE 109 ---

[BODY] might redeem us from death. If we desire to be partakers of this benefit, we must cultivate similar affections toward our neighbors. Not that any of us has reached such high perfection, but all must aim and strive according to the measure of their ability.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">An offering and a sacrifice to God of a sweet smelling savor.</sty> While this statement leads us to admire the grace of Christ, it bears directly on the present subject. No language, indeed, can fully represent the consequences and efficacy of Christ’s death. This is the only price by which we are reconciled to God. The doctrine of faith on this subject holds the highest rank. But the more extraordinary the discoveries which have reached us of the Redeemer’s kindness, the more strongly are we bound to his service. Besides, we may infer from Paul’s words, that, unless we love one another, none of our duties will be acceptable in the sight of God. If the reconciliation of men, effected by Christ, was <sty c="000000" i="1">a sacrifice of a sweet</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">smelling savor, </sty><sty c="800000" i="0">F49</sty> we, too, shall be “unto God a sweet savor,” (<sty c="800000" i="0"><470215></sty>2 Corinthians 2:15,) when this holy perfume is spread over us. To this applies the saying of Christ,

[CENTERED] “<sty c="000080" i="0">Leave thy gift before the altar, and go and be reconciled to thy brother.</sty>”
[CENTERED] <sty c="000080" i="0">(</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"><400524></sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Matthew 5:24.)</sty>

--- PAGE 110 ---

[H2] EPHESIANS 5:3-7
[INDENT] 3. But fornication, and all uncleanness, or covetousness, let it not be once named among you, as becometh saints;
[RIGHT] 3. Scortatio vero et omnis immundities, aut avaritia, ne nominentur quidem inter vos; sicut decet sanctos.

[INDENT] 4. Neither filthiness, nor foolish talking, nor jesting, which are not convenient; but rather giving of thanks.
[INDENT] 4. Turpitudo, stultiloquium, facetia; quae non conveniunt, sed magis gratia. 5. For this ye know, that no whoremonger, nor unclean person, nor covetous man, who is an idolater, hath any inheritance in the kingdom of Christ and of God.
[INDENT] 5. Hoc enim scitis, quod omnis scortator, vel immundus, vel avarus, qui est idololatra, non obtinebit haereditatem in regno Christi et Dei. 6. Let no man deceive you with vain words: for because of these things cometh the wrath of God upon the children of disobedience.
[INDENT] 6. Nemo vos decipiat inanibus verbis; nam propter haec venit ira Dei in filios inobedientes (vel, incredulos.) 7. Be not ye therefore partakers with them.
[RIGHT] 7. Ne sitis igitur illorum consortes.

[BODY] 3. <sty c="800000" i="1">But fornication.</sty> This chapter, and the third of the Epistle to the Colossians, contain many parallel passages, which an intelligent reader will be at no loss to compare without my assistance. Three things are here enumerated, which the apostle desires Christians to hold in such abhorrence, that they shall not even be named, or, in other words, shall be entirely unknown among them. By <sty c="800000" i="1">uncleanness</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"> </sty>he means all base and impure lusts; so that this word differs from <sty c="800000" i="1">fornication,</sty> only as the whole class differs from a single department. The third is <sty c="800000" i="1">covetousness,</sty> which is nothing more than an immoderate desire of gain. To this precept he adds the authoritative declaration, that he demands nothing from them but that which <sty c="800000" i="1">becometh saints,</sty> — manifestly excluding from the number and fellowship of the saints all fornicators, and impure and covetous persons.

[BODY] 4. <sty c="800000" i="1">Neither filthiness.</sty> To those three — other three are now added. By <sty c="800000" i="1">filthiness</sty> I understand all that is indecent or inconsistent with the modesty of the godly. By <sty c="800000" i="1">foolish talking</sty> I understand conversations that are either

--- PAGE 111 ---

[BODY] unprofitably or wickedly foolish; and as it frequently happens that idle talk is concealed under the garb of <sty c="000000" i="1">jesting</sty> or wit, he expressly mentions pleasantry, — which is so agreeable as to seem worthy of commendation, — and condemns it as a part of <sty c="800000" i="1">foolish talking</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">.</sty> The Greek word <sty c="0000d4" i="0">eujtrapeli>a</sty> is often used by heathen writers, in a good sense, for that ready and ingenious pleasantry in which able and intelligent men may properly indulge. But as it is exceedingly difficult to be witty without becoming satirical, and as jesting itself carries in it a portion of conceit not at all in keeping with the character of a godly man, Paul very properly dissuades from this practice. <sty c="800000" i="0">F50</sty> Of all the three offenses now mentioned, Paul declares that they are <sty c="800000" i="1">not convenient</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">,</sty> or, in other words, that they are inconsistent with Christian duty.

[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">But rather grace.</sty> Others render it <sty c="800000" i="1">giving of thanks;</sty> but I prefer Jerome’s interpretation. With the vices which had been formerly mentioned it was proper that Paul should contrast something of a general character, displaying itself in all our communications with each other. If he had said, “While they take pleasure in idle or abusive talk, do you give thanks to God,” the exhortation would have been too limited. The Greek word, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">eujcaristi>a</sty>, though it usually signifies <sty c="000000" i="1">Thanksgiving,</sty> admits of being translated <sty c="000000" i="1">Grace.</sty> “All our conversations ought to be, in the true sense of the words, sweet and graceful; and this end will be gained if the useful and the agreeable are properly mingled.”

[BODY] 5. <sty c="800000" i="1">For this ye know.</sty> If his readers were at all captivated by the allurements of those vices which have been enumerated, the consequence would be that they would lend a hesitating or careless ear to his admonitions. He determines, therefore, to alarm them by this weighty and dreadful threatening, that such vices shut against us the kingdom of God. By appealing to their own knowledge, he intimates that this was no doubtful matter. Some might think it harsh, or inconsistent with the Divine goodness, that all who have incurred the guilt of fornication or covetousness are excluded from the inheritance of the kingdom of heaven. But the answer is easy. Paul does not say that those who have fallen into those sins, and recovered from them, are not pardoned, but pronounces sentence on the sins themselves. After addressing the Corinthians in the same language, he adds:

--- PAGE 112 ---

[BODY] “<sty c="000080" i="0">And such were some of you; but ye are washed, but ye are</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">sanctified, but ye are justified, in the name of the Lord Jesus, and</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">by the Spirit of our God.</sty>”<sty c="000080" i="0"> (</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"><460611></sty><sty c="000080" i="0">1 Corinthians</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">6:11.)</sty>

[BODY] When men have repented, and thus give evidence that they are reconciled to God, they are no longer the same persons that they formerly were. But let all fornicators, or unclean or covetous persons, so long as they continue such, be assured that they have no friendship with God, and are deprived of all hope of salvation. It is called <sty c="800000" i="1">the kingdom of Christ and of God,</sty> because God hath given it to his Son that we may obtain it through him.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">Nor covetous man, who is an idolater.</sty> “Covetousness,” as he says in another place, “is idolatry,” (<sty c="800000" i="0"><510305></sty>Colossians 3:5,) — not the idolatry which is so frequently condemned in Scripture, but one of a different description. All covetous men must deny God, and put wealth in his place; such is their blind greediness of wretched gain. But why does Paul attribute to covetousness alone what belongs equally to other carnal passions? In what respect is covetousness better entitled to this disgraceful name than ambition, or than a vain confidence in ourselves? I answer, that this disease is widely spread, and not a few minds have caught the infection. Nay, it is not reckoned a disease, but receives, on the contrary, very general commendation. This accounts for the harshness of Paul’s language, which arose from a desire to tear from our hearts the false view.

[BODY] 6.<sty c="800000" i="1"> Let no man deceive you.</sty> There have always been ungodly dogs, <sty c="800000" i="0">F51</sty> by whom the threatenings of the prophets were made the subject of merriment and ridicule. We find such characters in our own day. In all ages, indeed, Satan raises up sorcerers of this description, who endeavor by unholy scoffs to escape the Divine judgment, and who actually exercise a kind of fascination over consciences not sufficiently established in the fear of God. “This is a trivial fault. Fornication is viewed by God as a light matter. Under the law of grace God is not so cruel. He has not formed us so as to be our own executioners. The frailty of nature excuses us.” These and similar expressions are often used by the scoffers. Paul, on the contrary, exclaims that we must guard against that sophistry by which consciences are ensnared to their ruin.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">For because of these things cometh the wrath of God.</sty> If we consider the present tense to be here used, agreeably to the Hebrew idiom, for the

--- PAGE 113 ---

[BODY] future, these words are a threatening of the last judgment. But I agree with those who take the word <sty c="800000" i="1">cometh</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"> </sty>in all indefinite sense, — <sty c="800000" i="1">the word of God</sty> usually <sty c="800000" i="1">cometh</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">,</sty> — as reminding them of the ordinary judgments of God which were executed before their own eyes. And certainly, if we were not blind and slothful, there are sufficiently numerous examples by which God testifies that he is the just avenger of such crimes, — examples of the pouring out of divine indignation, privately against individuals, and publicly against cities, and kings, and nations.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">Upon the children of disobedience,</sty> — upon <sty c="000000" i="1">unbelievers</sty> or <sty c="000000" i="1">rebels.</sty> This expression must not be overlooked. Paul is now addressing believers, and his object is not so much to present alarming views of their own danger, as to rouse them to behold reflected in wicked men, as in mirrors, the dreadful judgments of God. God does not make himself an object of terror to his children, that they may avoid him, but does all that can be done in a fatherly manner, to draw them to himself. They ought to learn this lesson, not to involve themselves in a dangerous fellowship with the ungodly, whose ruin is thus foreseen.

[H2] <sty c="800000" i="0"><490508></sty>EPHESIANS 5:8-14
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 8|||EN|||For ye were sometimes darkness, but now are ye light in the Lord: walk as children of light;|||LA|||Eratis aliquando tenabrae; nunc autem lux in Domino; tanquam filii lucis ambulate;
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 9|||EN|||(For the fruit of the Spirit is in all goodness, and righteousness, and truth;)|||LA|||(Fructus enim lucis in omni bonitate, et justitia, et veritate:)
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 10|||EN|||Probantes, quid sit acceptum Deo.|||LA|||
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 11|||EN|||And have no fellowship with the unfruitful works of darkness, but rather reprove them.|||LA|||Et ne communicetis operibus infructuosis tenebrarum; quin potius etiam redarguitote.
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 12|||EN|||For it is a shame even to speak of those things which are done of them in secret.|||LA|||Quae enim clam fiunt ab illis, turpe est vel dicere.
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 13|||EN|||But all things that are reproved are made manifest by the light: for whatsoever doth make manifest is light.|||LA|||Omnia autem, dum coarguuntur, a luce manifestantur; omne enim quod manifestat lux est.
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 14|||EN|||Wherefore he saith, Awake thou that sleepest, and arise from the dead, and Christ shall give thee light.|||LA|||Quamobrem dicit: Surge qui dormis, et exsurge ex mortuis; et illucescet tibi Christus.

--- PAGE 114 ---

[BODY] 8. <sty c="800000" i="1">For ye were once darkness.</sty> The precepts which immediately follow derive greater weight from the motives with which they are mingled. Having spoken of unbelievers, and warned the Ephesians not to become partakers of their crimes and their destruction, he argues still further, that they ought to differ widely from the life and conduct of those men. At the same time, in order to guard them against ingratitude to God, he refreshes their remembrance of their own past life. “You ought,” he says, “to be very different persons from what you formerly were; for out of darkness God hath made you light.” <sty c="800000" i="1">Darkness</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"> </sty>is the name here given to the whole nature of man before regeneration; for, where the brightness of God does not shine, there is nothing but fearful darkness. <sty c="800000" i="1">Light,</sty> again, is the name given to those who are <sty c="000000" i="1">enlightened</sty> by the Spirit of God; for immediately afterwards in the same sense, he calls them <sty c="800000" i="1">children of light,</sty> and draws the inference, that they ought to walk in light, because by the mercy of God they had been rescued from darkness. Observe here, we are said to be<sty c="800000" i="1"> light</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">in the Lord,</sty> because, while we are out of Christ, all is under the dominion of Satan, whom we know to be the Prince of darkness.

[BODY] 9. <sty c="800000" i="1">For the fruit of the light. </sty><sty c="800000" i="0">F52</sty> This parenthesis is introduced, to point out the road in which the <sty c="800000" i="1">children of light</sty> ought to walk. A complete description is not given, but a few parts of a holy and pious life are introduced by way of example. To give them a general view of duty, their attention is again directed to the will of God. Whoever desires to live in a proper and safe manner, let him resolve to obey God, and to take his will as the rule. To regulate life entirely by his command is, as he says in another Epistle, <sty c="000000" i="1">a reasonable service,</sty> (<sty c="800000" i="0"><451201></sty>Romans 12:1,) or, as another inspired man expresses it, <sty c="000000" i="1">To obey is better than sacrifice.</sty> (<sty c="800000" i="0"><091522></sty>1 Samuel 15:22.) I wonder how the word Spirit (<sty c="0000d4" i="0">pneumatoj</sty>) has crept into many Greek manuscripts, as the other reading is more consistent, — <sty c="800000" i="1">the fruit of</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">the light</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">.</sty> Paul’s meaning indeed is not affected; for in either case it will be this, that believers must walk in the light, because they are “children of the light.” This is done, when they do not live according to their own will, but devote themselves entirely to obedience to God, when they undertake nothing but by his command. Besides, such obedience is testified by its fruits, such as <sty c="800000" i="1">goodness, righteousness, and truth.</sty>

--- PAGE 115 ---

[BODY] 11.<sty c="800000" i="1"> And have no fellowship.</sty> As “the children of light” dwell amidst the darkness, or, in other words, in the midst of “a perverse and crooked generation,” (<sty c="800000" i="0"><053205></sty>Deuteronomy 32:5,) — there is good reason for warning them to keep themselves apart from wicked actions. It is not enough that we do not, of our own accord, undertake anything wicked. We must beware of joining or assisting those who do wrong. In short, we must abstain from giving any consent, or advice, or approbation, or assistance; for in all these ways we <sty c="800000" i="1">have fellowship</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">.</sty> And lest any one should imagine that he has done his duty, merely by not conniving, he adds, <sty c="800000" i="1">but rather</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">reprove them. </sty><sty c="800000" i="0">F53</sty> Such a course is opposed to all dissimulation. Where a manifest offense is committed against God, every man will be eager to vindicate himself from any share in the guilt, but very few will guard against connivance; nearly all will practice some kind of dissimulation. But rather than the truth of God shall not remain unshaken, let a hundred worlds perish.

[BODY] The word <sty c="0000d4" i="0">ejle>gcein</sty>, which is translated <sty c="800000" i="1">reprove,</sty> answers to the metaphor of darkness; for it literally signifies to drag forth to the light what was formerly unknown. As ungodly men flatter themselves in their vices, (<sty c="800000" i="0"><193602></sty>Psalm 36:2,) and wish their crimes to be concealed, or to be reckoned virtues, Paul enjoins that they shall be <sty c="800000" i="1">reproved</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">.</sty> He calls them <sty c="800000" i="1">unfruitful;</sty> because they not only do no good, but are absolutely hurtful.

[BODY] 12. <sty c="800000" i="1">Which are done by them in secret.</sty> This shews the advantage of reproving the ungodly. If they do but escape the eyes of men, there is no crime, however shocking to be mentioned, which they will not perpetrate. To use a common proverb, “Night has no shame.” What is the reason of this? Sunk in the darkness of ignorance, they neither see their own baseness, nor think that it is seen by God and by angels. But let the torch of God’s word be brought forward, and their eyes are opened. Then they begin to blush and be ashamed. By their advices and reproofs the saints enlighten blind unbelievers, and drag forth from their concealment to the light of day those who were sunk in ignorance.

[BODY] When unbelievers keep the doors of their houses shut, and withdraw from the view of men, <sty c="800000" i="1">it is a shame even to speak of</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"> </sty>the baseness and wickedness with which they rush into all manner of licentiousness. Would they thus lay aside all shame, and give loose reins to their passions, if darkness did not give them courage, if they did not entertain the hope that

--- PAGE 116 ---

[BODY] what is hidden will pass unpunished? But do you, by reproving them, bring forward the light, that they may be ashamed of their own baseness. Such shame, arising from an acknowledgment of baseness, is the first step to repentance.

[CENTERED] <sty c="000080" i="0">“If there come in one that believeth not, or one unlearned, he is</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">convinced of all, he is judged of all; and thus are the secrets of his</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">heart made manifest; and so, falling down on his face, he worships</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">God” (</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"><461424></sty><sty c="000080" i="0">1 Corinthians</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">14:24,25.)</sty>

[BODY] It may be thought that the word is used here in an unusual acceptation. Erasmus, by substituting another word for<sty c="800000" i="1"> reprove,</sty> has destroyed the whole meaning; for Paul’s object is to shew that it will not be without advantage if the works of unbelievers are reproved.

[BODY] 13. <sty c="800000" i="1">But when all things are reproved.</sty> As the participle, (<sty c="0000d4" i="0">fanerou>menon</sty>,) which is translated,<sty c="800000" i="1"> that which doth make manifest</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">,</sty> is in the middle voice, it admits either of a passive or active signification. It may be either rendered, <sty c="800000" i="1">that which is made manifest</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">,</sty> or <sty c="000000" i="1">that which doth make manifest.</sty> If the passive signification, which is followed by the ancient translator, be preferred, the word <sty c="800000" i="1">light</sty> will denote, as formerly, <sty c="000000" i="1">that which gives light,</sty> and the meaning will be, that evil works, which had been concealed, will stand out to public view, when they have been <sty c="800000" i="1">made manifest</sty> by the word of God: If the participle be taken actively, there will still be two ways of expounding it: 1. Whatever manifests is light; 2. That which manifests anything or all things, is light; taking the singular as put for the plural number. There is no difficulty, as Erasmus dreaded, about the article; for the apostles are not in the habit of adhering very strictly to rule about placing every article, and even among elegant writers this mode of using it would be allowable. The context appears to me to shew clearly that this is Paul’s meaning. He had exhorted them to reprove the evil works of unbelievers, and thus to drag them out of darkness; and he now adds, that what he enjoins upon them is the proper business of light — <sty c="800000" i="1">to make</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">manifest</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">.</sty> It is Light, he says, which makes all things manifest; and hence it followed that they were unworthy of the name, if they did not bring to light what was involved in darkness.

[BODY] 14. <sty c="800000" i="1">Wherefore he saith.</sty> Interpreters are at great pains to discover the passage of Scripture which Paul appears to quote, and which is nowhere to be found. I shall state my opinion. He first exhibits Christ as speaking by

--- PAGE 117 ---

[BODY] his ministers; for this is the ordinary message which is every day delivered by preachers of the gospel. What other object do they propose than to raise the dead to life?

[BODY] <sty c="000080" i="0">“The hour is coming, and now is, when the dead shall hear the</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">voice of the Son of God, and they that hear shall live”</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">(</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"><430525></sty><sty c="000080" i="0">John</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">5:25.)</sty>

[BODY] Let us now attend to the context. “Unbelievers,” Paul had said, “must be reproved, that, being brought forth to the light, they may begin to acknowledge their wickedness.” He therefore represents Christ as uttering a voice which is constantly heard in the preaching of the gospel,

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">Arise, thou that sleepest.</sty> The allusion, I have no doubt, is to the prophecies which relate to Christ’s kingdom; such as that of Isaiah ,

[CENTERED] “<sty c="000080" i="0">Arise, shine; for thy light is come, and the glory of Jehovah</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">is risen upon thee</sty>”<sty c="000080" i="0"> (</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"><236001></sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Isaiah 60:1.)</sty>

[BODY] Let us therefore endeavor, as far as lies in our power, to rouse the sleeping and dead, that we may bring them to the light of Christ.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">And Christ shall give thee light.</sty> This does not mean that, when we have risen from death to life, his light begins to shine upon us, as if our performances came before his grace. All that is intended is to show that, when Christ enlightens us, we rise from death to life, — and thus to confirm the former statement, that unbelievers must be recovered from their blindness, in order to be saved. Instead of <sty c="0000d4" i="0">ejpifau>sei</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">he</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">shall give</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">light,</sty> some copies read <sty c="0000d4" i="0">ejfa>uetai</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">he shall touch;</sty> but this reading is an evident blunder, and may be dismissed without any argument. <sty c="800000" i="0">F54</sty>

[H2] <sty c="800000" i="0"><490515></sty>EPHESIANS 5:15-20

[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 15|||EN|||See then that ye walk circumspectly, not as fools, but as wise|||LA|||Videte igitur, quomodo exacte ambuletis; non tanquam insipientes, sed tanquam sapientes:
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 16|||EN|||Redeeming the time, because the days are evil.|||LA|||Redimentes tempus, quoniam dies mali sunt.
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 17|||EN|||Wherefore be ye not unwise, but understanding what the will of the Lord is.|||LA|||Quare ne sitis imprudentes, sed intelligentes, quae sit voluntas Domini.
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 18|||EN|||And be not drunk with wine, wherein is excess; but be filled with the Spirit;|||LA|||Et ne inebriemini vino, in quo inest lascivia, sed impleamini Spiritu.
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 19|||EN|||Speaking to yourselves in psalms, and hymns, and spiritual songs, singing and making melody in your heart to the Lord;|||LA|||Vobis ipsis loquentes psalmis et hymnis, et canticis spiritualibus, canentes et psallentes in corde vestro, Domino;
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 20|||EN|||Giving thanks always for all things unto God and the Father in the name of our Lord Jesus Christ.|||LA|||Gratias agentes semper de omnibus, in nomine Domini nostri Iesu Christi, Deo et Patri.
[BODY] 15. <sty c="800000" i="1">See then.</sty> If believers must not neglect to drive away the darkness of others by their own brightness, how much less ought they to be blind as to their own conduct in life? What darkness shall conceal those on whom Christ, the Sun of righteousness, has arisen? Placed, as it were, in a crowded theater, they ought to live under the eye of God and of angels. Let them stand in awe of these witnesses, though they may be concealed from the view of all mortals. Dismissing the metaphor of darkness and light, he enjoins them to regulate their life <sty c="800000" i="1">circumspectly as wise men, </sty><sty c="800000" i="0">F55</sty> who have been educated by the Lord in the school of true wisdom. Our understanding must shew itself by taking God for our guide and instructor, to teach us his own will.

[BODY] 16. <sty c="800000" i="1">Redeeming the time.</sty> By a consideration of the time he enforces his exhortation.<sty c="800000" i="1"> The days are evil.</sty> Everything around us tends to corrupt and mislead; so that it is difficult for godly persons, who walk among so many

--- PAGE 119 ---

[BODY] thorns, to escape unhurt. Such corruption having infected the age, the devil appears to have obtained tyrannical sway; so that <sty c="800000" i="1">time</sty> cannot be dedicated to God without being in some way <sty c="800000" i="1">redeemed.</sty> And what shall be the price of its redemption? To withdraw from the endless variety of allurements which would easily lead us astray; to rid ourselves from the cares and pleasures of the world; and, in a word, to abandon every hinderance. Let us be eager to recover it in every possible way, and let the numerous offenses and arduous toil, which many are in the habit of alleging as an apology for indolence, serve rather to awaken our vigilance.

[BODY] 17. <sty c="800000" i="1">Wherefore be ye not unwise. </sty>He whose

[CENTERED] “<sty c="000080" i="0">delight is in the law of the Lord, and who meditates in it day and night,</sty>”
[CENTERED] <sty c="000080" i="0">(</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"><190102></sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Psalm 1:2,)</sty>

[BODY] will triumph over every obstacle which Satan can oppose to his progress. Whence comes it that some wander, others fall, others strike against a rock, others go away, — but because we allow ourselves to be gradually blinded by Satan, and lose sight of the will of God, which we ought constantly to remember? And observe, that Paul defines <sty c="800000" i="1">wisdom</sty> to be, <sty c="800000" i="1">understanding</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">what the will of the Lord is</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">.</sty>

[CENTERED] “<sty c="000080" i="0">How shall a young man,</sty>”<sty c="000080" i="0"> says David, </sty>“<sty c="000080" i="0">direct his way? By</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">attending to thy word, O Lord.</sty>”<sty c="000080" i="0"> (</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"><19B909></sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Psalm 119:9.)</sty>

[CENTERED] He speaks of youths, but it is the same wisdom which belongs to old men.

[BODY] 18. <sty c="800000" i="1">And be not drunk with wine.</sty> When he enjoins them <sty c="800000" i="1">not to be drunk,</sty> he forbids excessive and immoderate drinking of every description. “Be not intemperate in drinking.”

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">In which</sty> <sty c="800000" i="0">F56</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">is lasciviousness.</sty> The Greek word <sty c="0000d4" i="0">ajswti>a</sty>, which is translated “lasciviousness,” points out the evils which arise from drunkenness. I understand by it all that is implied in a wanton and dissolute life; for to translate it <sty c="000000" i="1">luxury,</sty> would quite enfeeble the sense. The meaning therefore is, that drunkards throw off quickly every restraint of modesty or shame; that where wine reigns, profligacy naturally follows; and consequently, that all who have any regard to moderation or decency ought to avoid and abhor drunkenness.

[BODY] The children of this world are accustomed to indulge in deep drinking as an excitement to mirth. Such carnal excitement is contrasted with that holy

--- PAGE 120 ---

[BODY] joy of which the Spirit of God is the Author, and which produces entirely opposite effects. To what does drunkenness lead? To unbounded licentiousness, — to unbridled, indecent merriment. And to what does spiritual joy lead, when it is most strongly excited? <sty c="800000" i="0">F57</sty>

[BODY] 19. <sty c="800000" i="1">To psalms, and hymns, and spiritual songs.</sty> These are truly pleasant and delightful fruits. The <sty c="800000" i="1">Spirit</sty> means “joy in the Holy Ghost,” (<sty c="800000" i="0"><451417></sty>Romans 14:17;) and the exhortation, <sty c="800000" i="1">be ye filled</sty>, (Ephesians 5.18,) alludes to deep drinking, with which it is indirectly contrasted<sty c="800000" i="1">. Speaking to</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">themselves,</sty> is <sty c="000000" i="1">speaking among themselves.</sty> Nor does he enjoin them to sing inwardly or alone; for he immediately adds, <sty c="800000" i="1">singing in your hearts;</sty> as if he had said, “Let your praises be not merely on the tongue, as hypocrites do, but from the heart.” What may be the exact difference between <sty c="800000" i="1">psalms</sty> and <sty c="800000" i="1">hymns</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">,</sty> or between <sty c="800000" i="1">hymns</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"> </sty>and <sty c="800000" i="1">songs</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">,</sty> it is not easy to determine, though a few remarks on this subject shall be offered on a future occasion. <sty c="800000" i="0">F58</sty> The appellation <sty c="800000" i="1">spiritual</sty><sty c="000000" i="1">,</sty> given to these songs, is strikingly appropriate; for the songs most frequently used are almost always on trifling subjects, and very far from being chaste.

[BODY] 20.<sty c="800000" i="1"> Giving thanks always.</sty> He means that this is a pleasure which ought never to lose its relish; that this is an exercise of which we ought never to weary. Innumerable benefits which we receive from God yield fresh cause of joy and thanksgiving. At the same time, he reminds believers that it will argue ungodly and disgraceful sloth, if they shall not <sty c="800000" i="1">always</sty> give thanks, — if their whole life shall not be spent in the study and exercise of praising God.

--- PAGE 121 ---

[H2] <sty c="800000" i="0"><490521></sty>EPHESIANS 5:21-27


[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 21|||EN|||Submitting yourselves one to another in the fear of God.|||LA|||Subditi estote invicem in timore Christi (vel, Dei.)
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 22|||EN|||Wives, submit yourselves unto your own husbands, as unto the Lord.|||LA|||Mulieres suis maritis subditae sint tanquam Domino;
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 23|||EN|||For the husband is the head of the wife, even as Christ is the head of the church; and he is the savior of the body.|||LA|||Quoniam vir est caput uxoris, quemadmodum et Christus caput est Ecclesiae, qui idem est servator corporis.
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 24|||EN|||Therefore, as the church is subject unto Christ, so let the wives be to their own husbands in every thing.|||LA|||Caeterum quemadmodum Ecclesia subest Christo, ita et mulieres suis maritis in omnibus.
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 25|||EN|||Husbands, love your wives, even as Christ also loved the church, and gave himself for it;|||LA|||Viri, diligite vestras uxores; quemadmodum et Christus dilexit Ecclesiam, et se ipsum tradidit pro ea
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 26|||EN|||That he might sanctify and cleanse it with the washing of water by the word;|||LA|||Ut eam sanctificaret, mundans lavacro aquae in Verbo;
[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 27|||EN|||That he might present it to himself a glorious church, not having spot, or wrinkle, or any such thing; but that it should be holy, and without blemish.|||LA|||Ut sisteret eam sibi gloriosam Ecclesiam, non habentem maculam, aut rugam, aut quicquam tale; sed ut esset sancta et irreprehensibilis.
[BODY] 21. <sty c="800000" i="1">Submit yourselves.</sty> God has bound us so strongly to each other, that no man ought to endeavor to avoid subjection; and where love reigns, mutual services will be rendered. I do not except even kings and governors, whose very authority is held for the <sty c="000000" i="1">service</sty> of the community. It is highly proper that all should be exhorted to be subject to each other in their turn.

[BODY] But as nothing is more irksome to the mind of man than this mutual subjection, he directs us to <sty c="800000" i="1">the fear of Christ,</sty><sty c="800000" i="0"> </sty>who alone can subdue our fierceness, that we may not refuse the yoke, and can humble our pride, that we may not be ashamed of serving our neighbors. It does not much affect the sense, whether we interpret <sty c="800000" i="1">the fear of Christ,</sty> passively, thus,

--- PAGE 122 ---

[BODY] — let us submit to our neighbors, because we fear Christ; or actively, let us submit to them, because the minds of all godly persons ought to be influenced by such fear under the reign of Christ. Some Greek manuscripts read, “the fear of <sty c="000000" i="1">God.</sty>” The change may have been introduced by some person, who thought that the other phrase, <sty c="000000" i="1">the fear of Christ,</sty> though by far the most appropriate, sounded a little harsh. <sty c="800000" i="0">F59</sty>
