[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">CHAPTER 5</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">1</sty> Be ye therefore imitators of God, as beloved children;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">2</sty> And walk in love, as Christ also hath loved us, and hath given himself for us an offering and sacrifice to God, for a smell of delightful fragrance.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">3</sty> But fornication, and all uncleanness, or covetousness, let <sty c="800000" i="0">4</sty>
[BODY] them not even be named among you, as becometh <sty c="000000" i="1">saints.</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">4</sty> Or filthiness, foolish talking, jesting; which are not convenient; but rather grace.<sty c="800000" i="0"> f126</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">5</sty> For this ye know, that no fornicator, or unclean person, or covetous man, who is an idolater, shall obtain inheritance in the kingdom of Christ and of God.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">6</sty> Let no man deceive you by vain words; for on account of these things cometh the wrath of God on disobedient <sty c="000000" i="1">(or, unbelieving)</sty> children,<sty c="800000" i="0"> f127</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">7</sty> Be not ye therefore their companions.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">8</sty> Ye were once darkness; but now ye are light in the Lord; walk as children of light;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">9</sty> (For the fruit of the light is in all goodness, and righteousness, and truth;)

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">10</sty> Proving what is acceptable to God.<sty c="800000" i="0"> f128</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">11</sty> And have no fellowship with the unfruitful works of darkness; but rather reprove them.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">12</sty> For it is a shame even to speak of those things which are done by them in secret.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">13</sty> But all things, when they are brought forward, are made manifest by the light; for every thing that maketh manifest is light.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">14</sty> Wherefore he saith: Awake, thou that sleepest, and arise from the dead; and Christ shall give thee light.

--- PAGE 160 ---

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">15</sty> See then how you walk carefully, not as fools, but as wise;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">16</sty> Redeeming the time, because the days are evil.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">17</sty> Wherefore be ye not <sty c="000000" i="1">imprudent, </sty>but understanding what is the will of the Lord.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">18</sty> And be not drunk with wine, in which is lasciviousness;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">19</sty> But be filled with the Spirit; speaking to yourselves in psalms, and hymns, and spiritual songs, singing and making melody in your heart, to the Lord;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">20</sty> Giving thanks always for all things, in the name of our Lord Jesus Christ, to God and the Father.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">21</sty> Submit yourselves to one another in the fear of Christ <sty c="000000" i="1">(or, of</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">God).</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">22</sty> Let wives be subject to their own husbands as to the Lord;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">23</sty> For the husband is the head of the wife, as Christ also is the savior of the church; and he is the savior of the body.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">24</sty> But, as the church is subject to Christ, in like manner (let) wives (he subject) to their own husbands in all things.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">25</sty> Husbands, love your wives; as Christ also loved the church, and gave himself for it,

[CENTERED] <sty c="800000" i="0">26</sty> That he might sanctify it, cleansing it with the washing of water by the word;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">27</sty> That he might present it to himself a glorious church, not having spot, or wrinkle, or any such thing; but that it might be holy and unblamable.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">28</sty> So ought husbands to love their wives, as their own bodies. He who loveth his wife loveth himself.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">29</sty> For no man ever hated his own flesh, but nourisheth and cherisheth it; as also Christ<sty c="800000" i="0"> f129</sty> the church;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">30</sty> For we are members of his body, of his flesh, and of his bones.

--- PAGE 161 ---

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">31</sty> For this reason shall a man leave his father and mother, and shall cleave to his wife; and the two shall be one flesh.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">32</sty> This is a great secret; but I speak concerning Christ and the church.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">33</sty> Yet let every one of you love his wife; and let the woman reverence her husband.

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">CHAPTER 6</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">1</sty> Children, obey your parents in the Lord; for this is right.

[CENTERED] <sty c="800000" i="0">2</sty> Honour thy father and mother; (which is the first commandment with promise;)

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">3</sty> That it may be well with thee, and that thou mayest be longlived on the earth.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">4</sty> Ye fathers also, provoke not your children to wrath; but bring them up in the instruction and reproof of the Lord.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">5</sty> Servants, obey your masters<sty c="800000" i="0"> f130</sty> according to the flesh, with fear and trembling, in the simplicity of your hearts, as to Christ;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">6</sty> Not with eye-service, as endeavoring to please men, but as the servants of Christ, doing the will of God from the heart:<sty c="800000" i="0"> f131</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">7</sty> With good-will, serving the Lord, and not men;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">8</sty> Knowing that everyone, whether he be bond or free, shall receive from the Lord whatever good he shall have done.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">9</sty> And ye, masters, perform your mutual duty toward them, forbearing threatenings; knowing that their Master and yours is in heaven; and there is no respect of persons with him.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">10</sty> Finally, my brethren, be strong in the Lord, and in the power of his might.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">11</sty> Put on the whole armor of God, that ye may be able to stand against the crafty devices of the devil.

--- PAGE 162 ---

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">12</sty> For we wrestle not against flesh and blood, but against principalities, against powers, against the princes of the world, of the darkness of this age, against spiritual wickednesses in heavenly places.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">13</sty> Wherefore take to you the whole armor of God, that ye may be able to resist in the evil day, and, having accomplished everything: to stand.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">14</sty> Stand therefore, having your loins girt with truth, and having put on the breastplate of righteousness,

[CENTERED] <sty c="800000" i="0">15</sty> And having your feet shod with the preparation of the gospel of peace;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">16</sty> Above all, taking the shield of faith, by which ye may be able to quench all the fiery darts of the wicked one.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">17</sty> And take the helmet of salvation, and the sword of the Spirit, which is the word of God;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">18</sty> Praying at all times in the Spirit, with all prayer and supplication, and watching for this very purpose, with all perseverance and supplication for all saints;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">19</sty> And for me, that speech may be given to me, in the opening of my mouth with boldness, that I may make known the mystery of the gospel;<sty c="800000" i="0"> f132</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">20</sty> For which I discharge the office of ambassador, in the chain; that I may conduct myself in it courageously, as I ought to speak.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">21</sty> But that ye also may know my affairs, what I do, Tychicus, a beloved brother and faithful minister in the Lord, will make known to you all things;

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">22</sty> Whom I have sent to you for that purpose, that ye might know my affairs, and might comfort your hearts.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">23</sty> Peace (be) to the brethren, and love, with faith, from God the Father and the Lord Jesus Christ.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">24</sty> Grace (be)with all who love our Lord Jesus Christ in sincerity. Amen.

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft5</sty> “Il nous a rendu agreables.” “He hath made us acceptable.”
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft6</sty> “The original word, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">ejcari>twsen</sty>, ‘he hath made us accepted,’ is not
[BODY] used by any profane authors; however, the sense of it is plain. It is used in the angel’s salutation to the Virgin Mary, ‘Hail, thou that art highly favored;’ and that the word there is rightly rendered, is plain from the reason which the angel himself gives, ‘Thou hast found favor with God.’ (<sty c="800000" i="0"><420128></sty>Luke 1:28,30) So that the plain meaning of the word, and the true rendering of it in the place before us, is, not as we have translated it, ‘made us accepted,’ but ‘highly favored us.’” — Chandler.
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft7</sty> “Comme si le sang de Christ sechoit et perdoit sa vigueur.” “As if the
[BODY] blood of Christ were dried up, and lost its force.”

[BODY] relative is attracted by the antecedent,) if, at least, we take <sty c="0000d4" i="0">ejperi>sseusen</sty>, with many modern expositors, in a <sty c="000000" i="1">neuter</sty> sense, ‘in which he hath renewed his abundant goodness to us;’ but if, with the ancient and some modern ones, in an active sense, ‘to make to abound,’

--- PAGE 164 ---

[CENTERED] (as in <sty c="800000" i="0"><470415></sty>2 Corinthians 4:15; 9:8,) the <sty c="0000d4" i="0">h=v</sty> will be for <sty c="0000d4" i="0">h[n</sty>, meaning, ‘which he has bountifully bestowed upon us.'“ — Bloomfield.
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft9</sty> <sty c="0000d4" i="0">jAnakefalaiw>sasqai</sty> “I have compared this word with
[BODY] <sty c="0000d4" i="0">sugkefalaiou~sqai</sty> in the writings of Xenophon, so as to bring out this sense, that ‘to Christ, as the Head, all things are subject.’ I am confirmed in this opinion by Chrysostom, who explains it in this manner: <sty c="0000d4" i="0">mi>an kefalh<n a[pasin ejpe>qhke to< kata< sa>rka Cristo>n</sty>, ‘he hath given to all one head, Christ according to the flesh.’ Polybius. also uses <sty c="0000d4" i="0">sugkeqalaiou~sqai</sty>, instead of <sty c="0000d4" i="0">ajnakeqalaiou~sqai</sty>. So that it is evident that those two words are employed indiscriminately.” — Raphelius.
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft10</sty> “The original word <sty c="0000d4" i="0">ajrjrJabw>n</sty>, seems properly to denote the first part
[BODY] of the price that is paid in any contract, as an earnest and security of the remainder, and which, therefore, is not taken back, but kept till the residue is paid to complete the whole sum. And thus it differs from a pledge, which is somewhat given for the security of a contract, but redeemed and restored, when the contract is completed; but it must be owned that the word is used to denote both an earnest and a pledge, and in either sense it is very properly applied to the Holy Spirit of promise.” — Chandler.
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft11</sty> “<sty c="000000" i="1">Louange</sty> yci se prend comme ci derant pour la publication et
[BODY] manifestation.” “Here, as formerly, ‘praise’ denotes proclamation and manifestation.”
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft12</sty> “‘Having heard of your faith in the Lord Jesus.’ It is wrong to argue
[BODY] from this expression, with Olshausen and De Wette, that the apostle had no personal knowledge of the persons whom he addressed. This was an early surmise, for it is referred to by Theodoret. Some, says he, have supposed that the apostle wrote to the Ephesians, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">wJv mhde>pw</sty> <sty c="0000d4" i="0">qeasa>menov aujtou<v</sty>, (as having never seen them.) But some years had elapsed since the apostle had visited Ephesus, and seen the Ephesian Church; and might he not refer to reports of their Christian steadfastness which had reached him? Nay, his use of the word may signify that such intelligence had been repeatedly brought to him.” — Eadie.
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft13</sty> “Il expose et esclarcit ce qu’il avoit dit ci-dessus.” “He explains and
[BODY] illustrates what he had formerly said.”

--- PAGE 165 ---

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft14</sty> Classical writers employ the same metaphor, to denote not spiritual
[BODY] death, with which they were unacquainted, but the absence of moral principle, or utter ignorance of right and wrong. Thus Epictetus says, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">nekro<v me<n oJ paideuth<v nekroi< d j uJmei~v o[te cortasqh~te</sty> <sty c="0000d4" i="0">sh>meron, kaqh~sqe klai>ontev peri< th~v au]rion po>qen fa>ghte.</sty> “The instructor is dead, and you are dead. When you are satiated today, you sit down and weep about to-morrow, what you shall have to eat.”<sty c="000000" i="1"> — Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft15</sty> “The Greek word <sty c="0000d4" i="0">aijw>n</sty>, and likewise the Latin word AEvum, both
[BODY] signify the ‘lip of man’ and from thence, by an easy figure, ‘the manner and custom’ of a person’s living; and therefore it denotes here the corrupt principles and morals, and particularly the idolatrous practices of the Heathen world, with which the Ephesians were as truly chargeable as the rest of mankind, before their conversion to the faith of Christ.” — Chandler.
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft16</sty> “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">Fu>siv</sty>, ‘nature,’ in such an idiom, signifies what is essential as
[BODY] opposed to what is accidental, what is innate in contrast with what is acquired. This is its general sense, whatever its specific application. Thus, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">Farma>kou fu>siv</sty> is the nature of a drug, its color, growth, and potency. <sty c="0000d4" i="0">Fu>sis tou~ Aijgu>ptou</sty> is the nature of the land of Egypt — a phrase referring to no artificial peculiarity, but to results which follow from its physical conformation.” — Eadie.
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft17</sty> “That is, exceedingly bountiful and liberal in the exercise of mercy. And
[BODY] in this metaphorical sense, the words ‘rich’ and ‘riches’ are used by the best writers. Lucian speaks of <sty c="0000d4" i="0">plou~tov filosofi>av</sty>, ‘the riches of philosophy.’ The Roman orator frequently speaks of the ‘riches of the mind,’ by which he means those excellencies of understanding and virtue which are the peculiar ornaments and riches of it. De Orat. I. So the apostle means here the infinite benignity of the Divine Nature, and his unchangeable disposition to be merciful.” — Chandler.
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft18</sty> “‘Loving with love,’ increaseth the emphasis and force of the
[BODY] expression. Cicero hath an expression exactly parallel: ‘Cura ut me ames amore illo tuo singulari.’ — E.p. Fam. ‘Be sure you love me with your singular and peculiar love.’ An allowed beauty in a profane author should not be censured as a tautology in a sacred one.” — Chandler.
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft19</sty> “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">Kai< tou~to oujk ejx uJmw~n</sty>. It has been not a little debated, among both

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft25</sty> According to that ancient prophecy, (Psalm 118:22,) ‘the stone, which
[BODY] the builders refused, is become the head-stone of the corner.’ The strength of buildings lies in their angles; and the corner-stone is that which unites and compacts the different sides of them; the chief cornerstone is that which is laid at the foundation, upon which the whole angle of the building rests, and which therefore is the principal support and tie of the whole edifice.” — Chandler.

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft30</sty> This seems to me plainly to allude, and to be urged in opposition to
[BODY] Diana of Ephesus, who was the common goddess of the Asiatic cities, in whose worship they were united, and by whose common contributions her temple was built, which was the common temple of those incorporated cities, so that all Asia (as we have it, <sty c="800000" i="0"><441927></sty>Acts 19:27) ‘worshipped her;’ which was therefore strictly and properly her family, over which she presided as the common mother and patroness; and there are models and ancient inscriptions remaining to this day, that abundantly prove it. Now the apostle tells these Ephesians, that, as Christians, they belonged to a nobler family, which took its denomination from, and was immediately subject to, God as a common Father; of whom the whole family in heaven and earth is named.” — Chandler.

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft50</sty> “He doth not condemn the innocent pleasantries and mirth of a cheerful
[BODY] conversation; but that kind of obscene discourse which we mean by the French expression of <sty c="000000" i="1">double entendre;</sty> when men, for the sake of merriment and sport, convey lewd sentiments and thoughts to others, under chaste and cleanly expressions. This seems to be the proper meaning of the word <sty c="0000d4" i="0">eujtrapeli>a</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">jesting,</sty> in this place. The original sense of it is, ‘an artfully turned discourse.’ And accordingly it is used either in a good sense, to denote proper wit; or in a bad sense, to signify any kind of lewd and scurrilous discourse, that artfully conveys an ill meanmg. And as it is here joined with ‘filthiness and foolish talking,’ it is plain that the apostle intended by it such ambiguous forms of speech as are intended to raise mirth by dishonest and corrupt meanings.” — Chandler.

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft61</sty> “One peculiarity in this injunction has been usually overlooked. What

The woman loves in deep, undying sympathy; but, to teach her how this fondness should know and fill its appropriate sphere, she is commanded to obey and honor. The man, on the other hand, feels that his position is to govern; but, to shew him what should be the essence and means of his government, he is enjoined to love.” — Eadie.

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft64</sty> “Plutarch tells us, (Symp. l. 2. page 638,) that wrestling was the most
[BODY] artful and subtle of all the ancient games, and that the name of it (<sty c="0000d4" i="0">pa>lh</sty>) was derived from a word, which signifies to throw a man down by deceit and craft. And it is certain that persons who understand this exercise have many fetches, and turns, and changes of posture, which they make use of to supplant and trip up their adversaries. And it is with great justice, that a state of persecution is compared with it; since many are the arts, arising from the terrors of worldly evil on the one

--- PAGE 173 ---

[BODY] hand, and the natural love which men have to life, liberty, plenty, and the pleasures of life, on the other, that the devil makes use of to circumvent and foil them.” — Chandler.
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft65</sty> “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">Pa>lh</sty> is properly a gymnastic term; but the Apostle often unites

Reisk, we have mention of Socrates wrestling with <sty c="000000" i="1">Melitus,</sty> with bonds and poison; next, the philosopher Plato wrestling with a tyrant’s anger, a rough sea, and the greatest dangers; then, Xenophon struggling with the prejudices of Tissaphernes, the snares of Ariaeus, the treachery of Meno, and royal machinations; and, lastly, Diogenes struggling with adversaries even more formidable, namely, poverty, infamy, hunger, and cold.” — Bloomfield.
